6
Conclusion

We develop a computationally efficient two-step estimator for nonlinear panel models with interactive unobserved effects, thereby helping bridge the gap between recent theory and empirical practice. Our procedure first solves a convex relaxation of the log-likelihood function via nuclear-norm regularization to obtain a consistent initial value, and then refines it with gradient descent on the log-likelihood function. We show that the log-likelihood function is locally convex in a shrinking neighborhood of the global optimum and establish a convergence rate for the first-step NNR estimator that is fast enough to enter this neighborhood, ensuring that the second step converges to the global minimum. Consequently, our final estimator is asymptotically equivalent to the fixed-effects estimator of
11
while avoiding the high-dimensional nonconvex optimization burden. This equivalence allows researchers to conduct inference using their theoretical results, making the estimator practically feasible.

Appendix A
Extensions and Technical Discussions

This section presents extensions and technical discussions of the assumptions in the main text: (1) We discuss the identification of fixed effects. (2) We show how to extend our analysis to the case where
X_{it}
contains predetermined variables. (3) We give easily verifiable sufficient conditions for restricted strong convexity (Assumption
2
) and diagonal structure (Assumption
3
) when predetermined covariates are present, along with new Monte Carlo simulation results for nonlinear panel models with predetermined covariates. (4) We demonstrate that our method remains applicable even when
\Sigma_{\lambda}\Sigma_{\gamma}
contains repeated eigenvalues (relaxing Assumption
1
(iv)
).

A.1
Identification of Fixed Effects

Similar to the linear case, we need additional
R^{2}
restrictions to identify fixed effects
(\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})

10
10

10

For any
R
-dimensional non-singular matrix
G
, the conditional distribution of
Y_{it}
remains invariant under the transformations
\lambda_{i}\mapsto\lambda_{i}G^{\prime}
and
\gamma_{t}\mapsto\gamma_{t}G^{-1}
. This invariance allows us to freely choose different normalization methods for different purposes without affecting the inference of
\beta_{0}
.

.
In Section
3
, we directly impose normalization constraints on fixed effects
(\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
such that

\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda/N
and
\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}\Gamma_{0}/T
are diagonal with
\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda/N=\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}\Gamma_{0}/T
. The feasibility of such normalization can be illustrated as follows: for any
(\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
satisfy Assumption
1
(iv)
, let
D
be the diagonal matrix containing the square root of the eigenvalues of the matrix
(NT)^{-1}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{1/2}\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}\Gamma_{0}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{1/2}
, and
\Upsilon
be the matrix of corresponding eigenvectors, then there exists a unique transformation matrix
G=D^{1/2}\Upsilon^{\prime}(\Lambda_{0}\Lambda_{0}/N)^{-1/2}
, such that the normalized nuisance parameters:

\displaystyle\Lambda^{G}_{0}:=\Lambda_{0}G^{\prime},\quad\Gamma^{G}_{0}:=\Gamma_{0}G^{-1}

lie in the normalized parameter space
\Phi_{NT}

11
11

11

When the eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{\lambda}\Sigma_{\gamma}
are distinct,
G
is unique and does not depend on samples
\{(Y_{it},X_{it})\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
. However, with possible repeated eigenvalues,
G
is not unique and can be identified up to orthogonal transformation.

. Unlike in the main text, in the following analysis and proofs, we distinguish between
(\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
and
(\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Gamma_{0}^{G})
to achieve the rigor of identification.

A.2
Predetermined Covariate

Our estimation method is applicable in scenarios where
X_{it}
includes both strictly exogenous covariates and predetermined variables (e.g., lags of
Y_{it}
in dynamic panels). While the main text focuses on the simpler case where
X_{it}
is strictly exogenous, this section extends the analysis to the more complex case involving predetermined variables. Considering that
X_{it}
includes predetermined variables is crucial in panel data because the time order is very important—–unlike in network data, where node order is irrelevant. Including these variables allows us to account for dynamic effects common in empirical research.

We partition
X_{it}
into
X_{it}:=(W^{\prime}_{it},Z^{\prime}_{it})^{\prime}
, where
W_{it}
represents
d_{W}
-dimensional predetermined covariates and
Z_{it}
represents
d_{Z}
-dimensional exogenous covariates. We collect
Z_{it,d}
into covariate matrix
Z_{d}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times T}
for each
d=1,2,\ldots,d_{Z}
, and let
Z
be the collection of all strictly exogenous covariate matrices,
Z=\{Z_{1},\ldots,Z_{d_{Z}}\}
. For each
i=1,2,\ldots,N
, denote
Y_{i}:=(Y_{i1},Y_{i2},\ldots,Y_{iT})^{\prime}
,
W_{i}=\left(W_{i1},W_{i2},\ldots,W_{iT}\right)^{\prime}
.
In addition, we use
\mathbb{P}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}=\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
to denote the conditional probability and use
\mathbb{E}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}=\mathbb{E}(\cdot\mid Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
to denote the conditional expectation.

Let us now consider the regularity conditions appropriate for predetermined covariates.

Assumption A.1
(Regularity Conditions - Predetermined Covariates)

Suppose that

(i)

(Sampling)
Conditional on
(Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
,
\{(Y_{i},W_{i})\}_{i=1,\ldots,N}
is independent across
i
, and for each
i
,
((Y_{i1},W_{i1}),(Y_{i2},W_{i2}),\ldots,(Y_{iT},W_{iT}))
is
\phi
-mixing with mixing coefficient
\phi_{i}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
, where

\displaystyle\phi_{i}(\tau)=\sup_{t}\sup_{A\in\mathcal{A}^{i}_{t},B\in\mathcal{B}^{i}_{t+\tau}}|\mathbb{P}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}(A\mid B)-\mathbb{P}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}(B)|

Here,
\mathcal{A}^{i}_{t}
is the sigma-field generated by
\{\ldots,(Y_{i,t-1},W_{i,t-1}),(Y_{i,t},W_{i,t})\}
, and
\mathcal{B}^{i}_{t+\tau}
is the sigma-field generated by
\{(Y_{i,t+\tau},W_{i,t+\tau}),(Y_{i,t+\tau+1},W_{i,t+\tau+1}),\ldots\}
. For mixing coefficients
\phi_{i}(\tau)
,
i=1,2,\ldots,N
, we further assume that they exhibit a uniformly exponential decay rate: there exists
\zeta_{0}>0
such that
\sup_{1\leq i\leq N}\phi_{i}(\tau)\leq e^{-\zeta_{0}\tau}
.

(ii)

(Compactness)
The parameter space of
\beta
,
\lambda_{i}
, and
\gamma_{t}
are bounded uniformly for all
i,t,N,T
. In addition, there exists constant
\rho_{X}>0
such that
\max_{d=1,\ldots,d_{X}}\|X_{d}\|_{\infty}<\rho_{X}
for all
i,t
and
N,T
.

(iii)

(Smoothness and Convexity)

-\ell_{it}(\cdot)
is four times continuously differentiable and strictly convex almost sure. Furthermore, we assume that
0<b_{\min}\leq-\ddot{\ell}_{it}(X_{it}^{\prime}\beta+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\gamma_{t})\leq b_{\max}<\infty
almost surely for all
\beta,\lambda_{i},\gamma_{t}
in the parameter space uniformly over
i,t,N,T
.

(iv)

(Strong Factor)
Assume that
\frac{1}{N}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\lambda_{i}\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma_{\lambda}
and
\frac{1}{T}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\gamma_{t}\gamma_{t}^{\prime}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma_{\gamma}
with
\Sigma_{\lambda}>0
and
\Sigma_{\gamma}>0
. In addition,
\Sigma_{\lambda}\Sigma_{\gamma}
has distinct eigenvalues.

The new regularity assumption differs from the previous one (Assumption
1
) only in the sampling assumption. In Assumption
A.1
(i)
, we impose weak dependence on the sequence
(Y_{it},W_{it})
: conditional on
(Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
, the sequence is
\phi
-mixing with an exponential decay rate. Although this is stricter than necessary, we adopt it to align with the sufficient conditions for restricted strong convexity (RSC).
\phi
-mixing can be replaced with
\alpha
-mixing, and the exponential decay rate can be relaxed to a sufficiently fast polynomial decay, as in
19
. In addition, we do not need identical distribution or stationarity assumptions on the sequence.

We now give the new consistency results of NNR estimators extending Theorem
2
to incorporate predetermined covariates:

Theorem A.1

For any
\alpha>0
such that
\varphi_{NT}\geq(1+\alpha)\max\{\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|_{2},\sqrt{NT}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\mathrm{op}}\}
, with Assumption
A.1
and Assumption
2
, and
N,T\rightarrow\infty
, there are constants
c_{1},c_{2}>0
that do not depend on
N,T
such that wpa1:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\beta_{0}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{NT}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{F}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

In addition, wpa1:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda^{G}_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma^{G}_{0}|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{2}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

The following corollary extend Corollary
3
to include predetermined covariates is:

Corollary A.2

Under the conditions in Theorem
A.1
, let
\varphi_{NT}=O\left(\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)
, there are constants
c_{3},c_{4}>0
that do not depend on
N,T
such that wpa1:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\beta_{0}\|_{2}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{NT}}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{3}\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}

In addition, wpa1:

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda^{G}_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\|\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma^{G}_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{4}\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}

We state the new diagonal structure assumption which generalizes Assumption
3
:

Assumption A.2
(Diagonal structure)

The population Hessian at the normalized true parameters,
\mathbb{E}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}\mathcal{H}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Gamma^{G}_{0})
, admits diagonal block structure if there exist a constant
C
(does not depend on
N,T
) such that

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}\mathcal{H}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Gamma_{0}^{G})\geq C\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathbb{I}_{d_{X}},\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{I}_{NR},\frac{1}{T}\mathbb{I}_{TR}\right\}

The local convexity and the asymptotic equivalence can be extended to incorporate predetermined covariates.

Theorem A.3

Under Assumption
A.2
and the conditions of Corollary
A.2
, suppose
N,T
have the same order. Let
\delta_{NT}=\log(NT)\min\{N^{-3/8},T^{-3/8}\}
, and
\mathcal{B}_{\delta_{NT}}
be the neighborhood defined in (
3.5
). The following results hold:

(i)

the local optimization problem (
3.7
) is strictly convex wpa1;

(ii)

our two-step estimator is asymptotically equivalent to the FE estimator;

(iii)

the local optimization problem (
3.7
) is strongly convex wpa1 uniformly on
\mathcal{B}_{\delta_{NT}}
, i.e., there exists a constant
c_{5}>0
independent of
N,T
such that for any
(\beta,\Lambda,\Gamma)\in\mathcal{B}_{\delta_{NY}}
,

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{NT}(\beta,\Lambda,\Gamma)>c_{5}\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathbb{I}_{d_{X}},\frac{1}{N}\mathbb{I}_{NR},\frac{1}{T}\mathbb{I}_{TR}\right\},\quad\text{wpa1}

A.3
Further Discussion on RSC

We aim to provide a set of easily verifiable sufficient conditions for the restricted strong convexity (RSC) condition in the panel data setting. Given the critical role of RSC condition in low-rank estimation, establishing verifiable low-level conditions in panel data setting is important and may be of independent interest. While
13
offers sufficient conditions for verifying the RSC condition in the panel data context, their approach is limited to strictly exogenous covariates. We extend their proof strategy to accommodate predetermined covariates, thereby broadening the applicability of the RSC in panel data. It is worth noting that although we are focusing on low-rank estimation with homogeneous slope, our proof strategy is quite flexible and is applicable to more general settings. For example, it can be readily adapted to establish the RSC condition under heterogeneous slopes, as in the models considered by
13
and
24
.

Assumption A.3

There exists a sequence of random vectors
\{v_{it}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N,1\leqslant t\leqslant T}
, such that

(i)

(Conditional weak dependence)
conditional on
\mathcal{V}:=\left\{v_{it}\mid 1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T\right\}
,
\{X_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
is independent across
i
, and for each
i
,
(X_{i1},X_{i2},\ldots,X_{iT})
is
\phi
-mixing with mixing coefficient
\phi_{i}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
, We assume that they exhibit uniformly exponential decay rate, i.e., there exists
\zeta_{1}>0
such that
\sup_{1\leq i\leq N}\phi_{i}(\tau)\leq e^{-\zeta_{1}\tau}
.

(ii)

(Conditional variability)
conditional on
\mathcal{V}
, there exists
\kappa_{0}>0
such that the following inequality holds for any
N,T
:

\displaystyle\inf_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}\sigma_{\min}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}(X_{it}X_{it}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{V})&\mathbb{E}(X_{it}\mid\mathcal{V})\\
\mathbb{E}(X_{it}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{V})&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\geq\kappa_{0}

where
\sigma_{\min}(\cdot)
denotes the minimum eigenvalue of a matrix.

The first part of Assumption
A.4
states that while it is unrealistic to assume weak dependence of
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
across
t
due to the presence of common factors, we assume that the serial correlation can be significantly reduced by conditioning on a set of latent factors,
\mathcal{V}
, i.e., The second part of Assumption
A.4
makes sure that
X_{it}
cannot be fully explained by
\mathcal{V}
.

More specifically, Assumption
A.3
(i)
impose weakly dependence on the sequence
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
: conditional on common factors
\mathcal{V}
, the sequence is
\phi
-mixing with an exponential decay rate. The conditional weak dependence assumption is much milder than it appears, and here are some examples:

Example A.1
(Factor Structure)

Suppose
X_{it}=\lambda_{x}^{\prime}\gamma_{t}+\epsilon_{it}
where
\lambda_{x}^{\prime}\gamma_{t}
is the common factor and
\epsilon_{it}
is error. Let
v_{it}=\lambda_{x}^{\prime}\gamma_{t}
, the conditional weak dependence condition holds if
\{\epsilon_{it}\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
is independent across both
i
and
t
conditional on
\mathcal{V}
.

Example A.2
(Serial Correlation)

Allowing for serial correlation becomes necessary when the model includes predetermined covariates. Suppose
X_{it}=AX_{i,t-1}+Bv_{it}+\epsilon_{it}

where
A
and
B
are the coefficient matrices and
\epsilon_{it}
is innovation term. We further assume that
\psi_{\max}(A)<1
, where
\psi_{\max}(A)
the largest singular of
A
. Then conditional on
\mathcal{V}
, the sequence
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
is
\phi
-mixing with exponential decay rate if conditional on
\mathcal{V}
, the innovation term
\{\epsilon_{it}\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
are independent across both
i
and
t
, and are strictly exogenous.

Example A.3
(Non-separable Model)

A more interesting case is the non-separable model with serial correlation, i.e.
X_{it}=h(X_{i,t-1},v_{it})
. For example, consider the dynamic Logit panel, where
X_{it}=Y_{i,t-1}
:

\displaystyle X_{it}=1(X_{i,t-1}+\lambda_{0,i}^{\prime}\gamma_{0,t}+\epsilon_{it}>0)

Here,
\epsilon_{it}
follows standard logistic distribution. Let
v_{it}=\lambda_{0,i}^{\prime}\gamma_{0,t}
, assume that the innovation term
\{\epsilon_{it}\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
are independent across both
i
and
t
, and are strictly exogenous. By Assumption
A.1
(iii)
we can conclude that conditional on
\mathcal{V}
,
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
is a time-inhomogeneous Markov process with transition matrices whose entries are uniformly bounded away from zero. Furthermore, by verifying Doeblin’s condition, it follows that
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
is
\phi
-mixing with exponential decay rate. We recommend readers interested in nonlinear time series models to refer to
15
and
37
for more details.

The Assumption
A.3
(ii)
imposes that even after controlling for the set of latent factors,
\mathcal{V}
,
X_{it}
preserves sufficient variation. Notably, by applying Schur’s decomposition, we directly obtain:

\displaystyle\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}(X_{it}X_{it}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{V})&\mathbb{E}(X_{it}\mid\mathcal{V})\\
\mathbb{E}(X_{it}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{V})&1\end{pmatrix}>0\quad\Longleftrightarrow\quad\mathrm{Var}(X_{it}\mid\mathcal{V})>0

If
X_{it}
given
\mathcal{V}
is identical distributed, the conditional variability condition simplifies to
\mathrm{Var}(X_{it}\mid\mathcal{V})>0
. If we further assume that
X_{it}
admits an additive structure, this condition is satisfied if
\inf_{i,t,N,T}\mathrm{Var}(\epsilon_{it}\mid\mathcal{V})>0
. Assumption
A.3
(ii)
can be viewed as a generalized of these simpler cases.

Lemma A.4
(Sufficient condition for RSC)

Under Assumption
A.1
and Assumption
A.3
, the RSC condition (Assumption
2
) is satisfied.

A.4
Further discussion on Diagonal Structure

We will provide sufficient conditions for verifying Assumption
3
and Assumption
A.2
, which is critical in establishing local convexity in Theorem
4
and Theorem
A.3
.
Assumption
3
and Assumption
A.2
require that the population Hessian, evaluated at the normalized true parameters
(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Gamma^{G}_{0})
, is positive definite, with a minimum eigenvalue on the order of
(\max\{N,T\})^{-1}
. We will show that these two assumption are very weak, and under the strong factor condition, it suffices for
X_{it}
to exhibit weak dependence after accounting for the influence of common factors and to retain components that cannot be fully explained by these factors.

Assumption A.4

There exists a sequence of random vectors
\{u_{it}\}_{1\leqslant i\leqslant N,1\leqslant t\leqslant T}
, such that

(i)

(Conditional weak dependence)
conditional on
\mathcal{U}:=\left\{u_{it}\mid 1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T\right\}
,
\{X_{i}\}_{1\leq i\leq N}
is independent across
i
, and for each
i
,
(X_{i1},X_{i2},\ldots,X_{iT})
is
\phi
-mixing with mixing coefficient
\phi_{i}(\tau)\rightarrow 0
as
\tau\rightarrow\infty
, We assume that they exhibit uniformly exponential decay rate, i.e., there exists
\zeta_{2}>0
such that
\sup_{1\leq i\leq N}\phi_{i}(\tau)\leq e^{-\zeta_{2}\tau}
.

(ii)

(Conditional variability)
conditional on
\mathcal{U}
, there exists
0<\nu<1
such that the following inequality holds uniformly for all
i,t,N,T
:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}(\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}X_{it}\mid\mathcal{U})\mathbb{E}(\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}X_{it}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{U})\leq\nu\mathbb{E}(\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}\mid\mathcal{U})\mathbb{E}(\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}X_{it}X_{it}^{\prime}\mid\mathcal{U})

where
\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}=\ddot{\ell}_{it}(X_{it}^{\prime}\beta_{0}+\lambda_{0,i}^{\prime}\gamma_{0,t})
.

The first part of this assumption is identical to the conditional weak dependence stated in Assumption
A.4
(i)
. It is worth noting that the condition requiring
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
to be
\phi
-mixing with an exponential decay rate uniformly across
i
is stronger than necessary and is imposed here for consistency with Assumption
A.3
(i)
. In fact, this condition could be relaxed to
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
being
\alpha
-mixing with a polynomial decay rate, still uniformly across
i
. Assumption
A.4
(ii)
imposes very weak additional restriction on the model. To elaborate, let us use
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}}(\cdot)
to denote the conditional expectation
\mathbb{E}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{U})
, and first consider the most simple case where
\ell_{it}^{0}
is a constant (corresponding to a linear panel model) with
d_{X}=1
. In this case, it is straightforward to verify that Assumption
A.4
(ii)
is equivalent to
(\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}{X}_{it})^{2}\leq\nu\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}({X}^{2}_{it})
uniformly for all
i,t,N,T
. When extending the analysis to the nonlinear model with
d_{X}=1
, it follows (using Hölder’s inequality) that:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}}(\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}{X}_{it})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}}(|\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}{X}_{it}|)=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}}(|\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}|^{1/2}|\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}{X}_{it}|^{1/2})\leq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}}(-\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0})^{1/2}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{U}}(-\ddot{\ell}_{it}^{0}{X}^{2}_{it})^{1/2}

Equality holds if and only if
X_{it}
is a constant given
\mathcal{U}
, i.e.,
X_{it}
can be fully explained by
\mathcal{U}
. Thus, in this case, Assumption
A.4
(ii)
can be viewed as a uniform version of Hölder’s inequality. Extending the analysis from
d_{X}=1
to
d_{X}>1
is straightforward and yields the same conclusions.

Lemma A.5
(Sufficient conditions for diagonal structure)

Under Assumption
A.1
and Assumption
A.4
, Assumption
3
and Assumption
A.2
hold.

A.5
Additional Monte Carlo Simulations

We evaluate the performance of our estimator in a binary response model with predetermined covariates, where the data are generated from the following single-index model with
R=2
:

\displaystyle Y_{it}

\displaystyle=\boldsymbol{1}\left(\beta_{1}Y_{i,t-1}+\beta_{2}Z_{it}+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\gamma_{t}+\epsilon_{Y,it}>0\right)

(A.1)

\displaystyle Z_{it}

\displaystyle=\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\gamma_{t}+\lambda_{i}^{\prime}\iota+\gamma_{t}^{\prime}\iota+\lambda_{z,i}\gamma_{z,t}+\epsilon_{Z,it}

where
\lambda_{i}=(\lambda_{i1},\lambda_{i2})^{\prime}
,
\gamma_{t}=(\gamma_{t1},\gamma_{t2})^{\prime}
,
\{\lambda_{ir}\}_{\{1\leq i\leq N,r=1,2\}}
,
\{\gamma_{tr}\}_{\{1\leq t\leq T,r=1,2\}}
,
\{\lambda_{X,i}\}_{\{1\leq i\leq N\}}
, and
\{\gamma_{X,t}\}_{\{1\leq t\leq T\}}
are sets of independent random variables drawn from the standard normal distribution
N(0,1)
. The error terms
\{\epsilon_{Y,it}\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
are i.i.d. random variables over both
i
and
t
following the logistic distribution, and
\{\epsilon_{X,it}\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
are i.i.d. random variables over both
i
and
t
following normal distribution
N(0,4)
. We set
R_{\max}=5
and
(\beta_{1},\beta_{2})=(0.5,0.2)
. Conditional on
\Lambda_{0}
and
\Gamma_{0}
,
Z_{it}
is exogenous covariate while the lag of
Y_{it}
is predetermined variable.

Note:
Monte Carlo results based on
1000
repetitions for the Logit model with predetermined covariate (
A.1
). We report bias and standard deviation for pooled estimator (POOL), nuclear norm regularized estimator (NNR). Using
R=2
, we report bias and standard deviation for local fixed effect estimator (FE) and local fixed effect estimators after analytical bias correction (FE_A). Using
R=\hat{R}
factors, we report bias and standard deviation for local fixed effect estimators (FER) and local fixed effect estimators after analytical bias correction (FER_A). Furthermore, We report the mean of estimated number of factor
\overline{R}
.

We do not report the sample-splitting Jackknife bias correction, as it requires an additional homogeneity condition (
19
, Assumption 4.3), which we consider too restrictive in dynamic settings. For the analytical correction, we directly apply the analytical bias correction method proposed in
11
to the dynamic setting, despite their method not being specifically designed for dynamic models. Developing a valid analytical bias correction formula for dynamic nonlinear panel models with interactive fixed effects is challenging and beyond the scope of this paper.

We conduct
1000
Monte Carlo replication to evaluate the performance of our estimator in both small samples (e.g.,
N=50,T=40
) and large samples (e.g.,
N=1000,T=200
), and present the bias and standard deviation in Table
1
. The first column (POOL) presents the pooled regression results, which exhibit a large bias that does not diminish as the sample size increases. The second column (NNR) reports the performance of NNR estimator, whose bias decreases slowly to zero as
N,T\rightarrow\infty
, which is consistent with Theorem
A.1
. The bias of FE estimators is smaller than that of NNR estimators and converges quickly to zero. Notably, the bias and standard deviation of FE estimators remain of the same order even for large
N,T
due to the incidental parameter problem. The fourth column (FE_A) presents the results after applying analytical bias correction to the FE estimator. The analytical correction effectively reduces the bias across all sample sizes, with particularly notable improvements for large
N,T
, where the bias becomes negligible compared to the standard deviation. It is interesting to see that the estimation of bias corrected dynamic effect
\beta_{1}
, is much more accurate than
\beta_{2}
.

With the estimated number of factors
\hat{R}
, we evaluate the performance of the FE estimator (FER), and the analytical bias-corrected estimator (FER_A). Since the estimation of
R
is highly accurate even in small samples (the averages of
\hat{R}
are reported in the last column), the performance of these estimators based on
\hat{R}
is very similar to their performance when the true number of factors,
R=2
, is used. In some cases, the bias-corrected estimator using
\hat{R}
even performs better than the bias-corrected estimator that uses the true
R
. For example, in the case of
N=200,T=40
, the bias of
\hat{\beta}_{1}
for FEA is
-0.47
, while the bias for FER_A is
0.09
. We attribute this observation to small sample effects. However, such cases do not affect the evaluation of our two-step estimator, as the bias remains small relative to the standard deviation in both scenarios. In summary, similar to the Monte Carlo simulation results in Table
1
, our two-step estimator demonstrates excellent performance in large samples and remains highly reliable even in small samples.

A.6
Relaxing Distinct Eigenvalues Assumption

We now demonstrate that our method remains applicable even when
\Sigma_{\lambda}\Sigma_{\gamma}
contains repeated eigenvalues (relaxing Assumption
1
(iv)
and Assumption
A.1
(iv)
). When
\Sigma_{\lambda}\Sigma_{\gamma}
has distinct eigenvalues, there exists a unique
R
-dimensional invertible matrix,
G=D^{1/2}\Upsilon^{\prime}(\Lambda_{0}\Lambda_{0}/N)^{-1/2}
, which does not depend on the sample
\{(Y_{it},X_{it})\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
, such that
\Lambda^{G\prime}_{0}\Lambda^{G}_{0}/N,\Gamma^{G\prime}_{0}\Gamma^{G}_{0}/T
are diagonal and
\Lambda^{G\prime}_{0}\Lambda^{G}_{0}/N=\Gamma^{G\prime}_{0}\Gamma^{G}_{0}/T
. However, when
\Sigma_{\lambda}\Sigma_{\gamma}
has repeated eigenvalues, the transformation
G
may depend on the sample. In this case, we denote it by
\hat{G}
:
\hat{G}=D^{1/2}\hat{O}\Upsilon^{\prime}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0}/N)^{-1/2}
(see the proof of Theorem
A.1
), where
\hat{O}
is an orthogonal matrix that depends on the sample.

The dependence of
\hat{G}
on the sample adds extra complexity to the proof. Therefore, we aim to search for an objective function independent of
G
, along with its corresponding Hessian, allowing us to directly apply the proof of Theorem
A.3
to establish asymptotic equivalence.

Let us define

\displaystyle G_{NT}=\mathrm{diag}\left\{\mathbb{I}_{d_{X}},\underbrace{\hat{G},\ldots,\hat{G}}_{N},\underbrace{\hat{G}^{-1},\ldots,\hat{G}^{-1}}_{T}\right\}

We can verify that
\hat{G}
is invertible wpa1, the minimum eigenvalue of
\hat{G}
is strictly positive wpa1, and the maximum eigenvalue of
\hat{G}
is uniformly bounded wpa1 (see the proof of Theorem
A.1
). Therefore, it follows that
G_{NT}
is invertible wpa1, the minimum eigenvalue of
G_{NT}
is strictly positive wpa1, and the maximum eigenvalue of
G_{NT}
is uniformly bounded wpa1. In addition, one can easily verify both

\displaystyle\nabla^{2}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})=G_{NT}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Gamma_{0}^{G})G_{NT}^{\prime-1}

and

G_{NT}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}^{\prime}\Lambda-\Gamma^{\prime}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}G_{NT}^{\prime-1}
do not depend on
G_{NT}
, then

\displaystyle\mathcal{H}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Gamma_{0}^{G})

\displaystyle=G_{NT}\nabla^{2}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})G_{NT}^{-1}+G_{NT}G_{NT}^{-1}\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}^{\prime}\Lambda-\Gamma^{\prime}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}G_{NT}^{\prime-1}G_{NT}^{\prime}

\displaystyle=G_{NT}\underbrace{\left(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})+G_{NT}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}^{\prime}\Lambda-\Gamma^{\prime}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}G_{NT}^{\prime-1}\right)}_{\text{does not depend on $\hat{G}$}}G_{NT}^{\prime}

Therefore,
\mathbb{E}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}\mathcal{H}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Gamma_{0}^{G})
satisfies Assumption
A.2
(or Assumption
3
) if and only if

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}\left(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})+G_{NT}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}^{\prime}\Lambda-\Gamma^{\prime}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}G_{NT}^{\prime-1}\right)

also satisfies Assumption
A.2
(or Assumption
3
). One can easily employ the same technique used in the proof of Lemma
A.5
to show that Assumption
A.1
and Assumption
A.4
are sufficient to verify that
\mathbb{E}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}\left(\nabla^{2}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})+G_{NT}^{-1}\nabla^{2}\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}^{\prime}\Lambda-\Gamma^{\prime}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}G_{NT}^{\prime-1}\right)
has block structure. Finally, by replacing
(\Lambda^{G}_{0},\Gamma_{0}^{G})
with
(\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
in the proof of Theorem
A.3
, Theorem
A.3
still holds even in the presence of repeated eigenvalues.

Appendix B
Proofs of Consistency of the NNR estimator

We introduce new notations for simplicity. For any two positive real sequences
\left\{a_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 1}
and
\left\{b_{n}\right\}_{n\geq 1}
, we use
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
(
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
) to denote that there exists a positive constant
c
such that
a_{n}\leq cb_{n}
(
a_{n}\geq cb_{n}
) for all
n
. We write
a_{n}\asymp b_{n}
if both
a_{n}\lesssim b_{n}
and
a_{n}\gtrsim b_{n}
. Recall that there are constants
(\rho_{\beta},\rho_{\lambda},\rho_{\gamma},\rho_{\theta})
such that

\displaystyle\|\beta\|_{\max}\leq\rho_{\beta},\quad\|\Lambda_{0}\|_{\max}\leq\rho_{\lambda},\quad\|\Gamma_{0}\|_{\max}\leq\rho_{\gamma},\quad\|\Theta\|_{\max}\leq\rho_{\theta},

We define the estimation errors as:

\displaystyle\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}=\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\beta_{0},\quad\hat{\Delta}_{\Lambda}=\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda_{0},\quad\hat{\Delta}_{\Gamma}=\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma_{0},\quad\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}=\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}

and it follows from (
3.1
) that

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|_{\max}\leq 2\rho_{\beta},\quad\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\max}\leq 2\rho_{\theta}

The following lemma contains basic properties of low-rank projections, which will be used frequently in the subsequent proof.

Lemma B.1

Let
\Delta
be an
N\times T
matrix, and define
M_{\Lambda_{0}}
and
M_{\Gamma_{0}}
as in (
3.2
). Then, the following properties hold:

(i)

\|\Delta\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Delta M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\|\Delta-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Delta M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}
.

(ii)

\|\Delta\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}=\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Delta M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}+\|\Delta-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Delta M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}
.

(iii)

\mathrm{rank}(\Delta-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Delta M_{\Gamma_{0}})\leq 2R
.

The proof is omitted, but readers may refer to Lemma D.2 from
13
and Chapter 10 in
38
for details.

B.1
Proofs of Theorem
2
and Theorem
A.1

We only provide the proof of Theorem
A.1
since Theorem
A.1
extends Theorem
2
to incorporate predetermined covariates.

Proof of Theorem
A.1
.

The proof is based on the following lemma:

Lemma B.2

Under conditions of Theorem
A.1
,

\displaystyle\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\leq\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\right)

where
M_{\Lambda_{0}}
and
M_{\Gamma_{0}}
are defined in (
3.2
).

The proof of Lemma
B.2
is standard and will be presented at the end of this subsection. Lemma
B.2
states that, when the penalization parameter
\varphi_{NT}
is sufficiently large, the component of the estimation error of
\Theta
that cannot be explained by neither
\Lambda_{0}
nor
\Gamma_{0}
is relatively small compared to the part of the estimation error of
\Theta
that can be explained by
\Lambda_{0}
and
\Gamma_{0}
, along with a term that accounts for the estimation error of
\beta
.

When

\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\sqrt{\frac{\log(NT)}{NT}}
, we can directly obtain

\displaystyle\|\Delta_{\beta}\|

\displaystyle\leq\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}},\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{NT}}\|\Delta_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}

(B.1)

When
\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}>\sqrt{\frac{\log(NT)}{NT}}
, the proof is more intricate and requires additional effort.

Step 1

Since
(\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
solves problem (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\beta},\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta})-\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\leq\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}(\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}})

(B.2)

Consider the Taylor expansion of
\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
around
(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\beta},\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta})-\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})-\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}-\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle

(B.3)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}\underbrace{(\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}})}_{\leq\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}}-\underbrace{\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}}_{\leq\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|}-\underbrace{\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle}_{\leq\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{1+\alpha}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{(1+\alpha)\sqrt{NT}}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{2\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}\left(\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|_{2}\right)

where inequality (i) follows from inequality (
B.2
), inequality (ii) employs triangular inequality, Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and Hölder inequality (since the spectral norm is the dual norm of the nuclear norm), and inequality (iii) holds because of
\varphi_{NT}\geq(1+\alpha)\max\{\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|,\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\}
as stated in Theorem
A.1
. In addition, we have the following inequalities regarding the nuclear norm:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}\left(\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\right)+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\frac{2(1+\alpha)\sqrt{2R}}{\alpha}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iv)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\frac{2(1+\alpha)\sqrt{2R}}{\alpha}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{2(1+\alpha)\sqrt{2R}}{\alpha}(\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}})

where equality (i) holds due to Lemma
B.1
(i)
, inequality (ii) follows from Lemma
B.2
, inequality (iii) follows Lemma
B.1
(iii)
,
and inequality (iv) is based on Lemma
B.1
(ii)
.

Therefore, combining the nuclear norm inequality and inequality (
B.3
) yields:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\beta},\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta})-\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})-\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}-\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle

(B.4)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{8(1+\alpha)\sqrt{2R}}{\alpha}\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}(\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}})

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{16(1+\alpha)\sqrt{2R}}{\alpha}\varphi_{NT}\sqrt{\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}}

Step 2

By the convexity of
\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\cdot,\cdot)
, we have:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\beta},\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\theta})-\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})-\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}-\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle

(B.5)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle\frac{1}{NT}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(-\ddot{\ell}(X_{it},\tilde{\beta},\tilde{\lambda}_{i},\tilde{\gamma}_{t}))(X_{it}^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}+\hat{\Delta}_{\theta_{it}})^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle b}_{\min}\frac{1}{NT}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(X_{it}^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}+\hat{\Delta}_{\theta_{it}})^{2}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{\geq}}

\displaystyle{\displaystyle b}_{\min}\left(\kappa\left(\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right)-\eta\frac{N+T}{NT}(\log(NT))^{2}\right)

where inequality (i) follows from the convexity of
\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\cdot,\cdot)
, inequality (ii) holds because
-\ddot{\ell}_{it}(\cdot)\geq b_{\min}
uniformly as stated in Assumption
A.1
(iii)
, and inequality (iii) employs Lemma
B.2
and RSC (Assumption
2
).

By combining (
B.4
) and (
B.5
), we obtain:

\displaystyle{b}_{\min}\left(\kappa\left(\|\Delta_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\Delta_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right)-\eta\frac{N+T}{NT}(\log(NT))^{2}\right)

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16(1+\alpha)\sqrt{2R}}{\alpha}\varphi_{NT}\sqrt{\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|^{2}_{\mathrm{F}}}

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\sqrt{\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{a_{1}\varphi_{NT}+\sqrt{a_{1}^{2}\varphi_{NT}^{2}+4a_{2}^{2}\frac{N+T}{NT}(\log(NT))^{2}}}{2}

\displaystyle\leq a_{1}\varphi_{NT}+a_{2}\sqrt{\frac{N+T}{NT}}(\log(NT))^{2}

\displaystyle\leq a_{1}\varphi_{NT}+a_{2}\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}

where
a_{1}=\frac{16(1+\alpha)\sqrt{2R}}{\alpha b_{\min}\kappa}>0
,
a_{2}=\frac{\eta}{{b}_{\min}\kappa}>0
. Let
c_{1}=\max\{a_{1},a_{2}\}
and we can prove that wpa1

\displaystyle\|\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\beta_{0}\|

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{NT}}\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq c_{1}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

Step 3

We aim to establish the estimation error bound for
\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
. The error bound for
\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
will be directly obtained by the same method. It is worth noting that our proof below is based on the situation where
\Sigma_{\lambda}^{1/2}\Sigma_{\gamma}\Sigma_{\lambda}^{1/2}
may have repeated eigenvalues.
For notational simplicity, let
\Upsilon
be the
R
-dimensional matrix containing eigenvectors of
\frac{1}{NT}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{1/2}\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}\Gamma_{0}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{1/2}
, let
D
be the
R
-dimensional diagonal matrix of square root of eigenvalues of
\frac{1}{NT}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{1/2}\Gamma_{0}^{\prime}\Gamma_{0}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{1/2}
,
\Omega
be the
R
-dimensional vector matrix containing square root of eigenvalues of
\Sigma_{\lambda}^{1/2}\Sigma_{\gamma}\Sigma_{\lambda}^{1/2}
in non-increasing order, and
U_{0}
be the left singular vector of
\Theta_{0}
. We can easily verify that
U_{0}=\Lambda_{0}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{-1/2}\Upsilon
. In addition, let
\hat{U}
be the matrix containing left singular vector of
\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
such that
\hat{U}^{\prime}\hat{U}=\mathbb{I}_{R}
, we directly have
\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\sqrt{N}\hat{U}\hat{D}^{1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}
.

We first establish the bound for the distance between two spaces spanned by
\hat{U}
and
U_{0}
, respectively. Using Davis-Kahan Theorem (Theorem 4 in
42
), there exists an orthogonal matrix
O^{*}
such that wpa1:

\displaystyle\|\hat{U}-U_{0}O^{*\prime}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{2^{\frac{3}{2}}(2\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}+\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}})\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\psi^{2}_{R}\left(\Theta_{0}\right)}

(B.6)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\frac{8\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\psi^{2}_{R}\left(\Theta_{0}\right)}

where inequality (i) follows from fact that
\psi_{R+1}(\Theta_{0})=0
, inequality (ii) is based on the estimation error bound of
\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
, implying that
\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}/\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}0
. The matrix
O^{*}
arises due to the possible multiplicity of eigenvalues and depends only on

(\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
. In addition, the strong factor assumption (Assumption
A.1
(iv)
) implies that:

\begin{gathered}\frac{1}{\sqrt{NT}}\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{op}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{1},\quad\frac{1}{\sqrt{NT}}\psi_{R}\left(\Theta_{0}\right)\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Omega_{R}\end{gathered}

(B.7)

Combining inequalities (
B.6
) and (
B.7
) gives that wpa1:

\displaystyle\|\hat{U}-U_{0}O^{*\prime}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{16\Omega_{1}}{\sqrt{NT}\Omega_{R}^{2}}\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\frac{16c_{1}\Omega_{1}}{\Omega_{R}^{2}}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right)

Now we turn to the error bound of
\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
. Note that:

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\sqrt{N}\underbrace{\Lambda_{0}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda_{0})^{-1/2}\Upsilon}_{U_{0}}O^{*\prime}D^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}=

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}\left\|\hat{U}\hat{D}^{1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}-U_{0}O^{*\prime}D^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}\left\|\hat{U}-U_{0}O^{*\prime}D^{1/2}\hat{D}^{-1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\left\|\hat{D}^{1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{N}\left(\underbrace{\left\|\hat{U}-U_{0}O^{*\prime}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{A_{1}}\left\|\hat{D}^{1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\underbrace{\left\|U_{0}O^{*\prime}\left(\hat{D}^{1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}-D^{1/2}\right)\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{A_{2}}\right)

We have already established the bound of
A_{1}
, let us now focus on the upper bound of
A_{2}
.
Since

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{D}^{1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}-D^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{R}\frac{\left\|\hat{D}_{[1:R,1:R]}-D\right\|_{\mathrm{op}}}{2\min\{\psi_{R}^{1/2}\left(\hat{D}_{[1:R,1:R]}\right),\psi_{R}^{1/2}\left(D\right)\}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\sqrt{R}\frac{\left\|\hat{D}_{[1:R,1:R]}-D\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{2\min\{\psi_{R}^{1/2}\left(\hat{D}_{[1:R,1:R]}\right),\psi_{R}^{1/2}\left(D\right)\}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{\sqrt{R}\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\sqrt{NT}\Omega_{R}^{1/2}}

the following inequality holds wpa1:

\displaystyle A_{2}\leq\underbrace{\|U_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{=\sqrt{R}}\left\|\hat{D}^{1/2}_{[1:R,1:R]}-D^{1/2}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq\frac{R\|\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}{\sqrt{NT}\Omega_{R}^{1/2}}\leq\frac{c_{1}R}{\Omega_{R}^{1/2}}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right)

Therefore, let
G=D^{1/2}O^{*}\Upsilon^{\prime}(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda/N)^{-1/2}
, we have

\displaystyle\left\|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda_{0}G^{\prime}\right\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{N}\underbrace{\left(\frac{16c_{1}\Omega_{1}^{3/2}}{\Omega_{R}^{2}}+\frac{c_{1}R}{\Omega_{R}^{1/2}}\right)}_{B_{1}}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right),\quad\text{wpa1}

By the same method, we show that

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma_{0}G^{-1}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{T}B_{1}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right),\quad\text{wpa1}

In addition, since

\displaystyle GG^{\prime}=\underbrace{D^{1/2}}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{diag}(\Omega)}O^{*}\Upsilon^{\prime}\underbrace{(\Lambda_{0}^{\prime}\Lambda/N)^{-1}}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\Sigma_{\lambda}^{-1}}\Upsilon O^{*\prime}\underbrace{D^{1/2}}_{\stackrel{{\scriptstyle p}}{{\longrightarrow}}\mathrm{diag}(\Omega)}

one can directly verify that, with probability approaching to
1
, the maximum singular value of
G
is uniform bounded, and the minimum singular value of
G
is strictly greater than zero. Therefore, we finish the proof of the theorem.

In the next step, we will discuss how to obtain a nuisance estimator with better properties (e.g., uniformly bounded) to ensure that
(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})\in\Phi_{NT}
. While this is not directly related to the current theorem, it will be frequently used in subsequent theoretical discussions.

Step 4

As we have discussed in the main text, one potential concern of constructing
(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
as in (
2.5
) is that the entries of
(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
are not necessarily uniformly bounded. In this step, we will show that after truncating and normalizing the estimators in (
2.5
), we can obtain new nuisance estimators
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
that satisfy the uniform boundedness condition, and consequently,
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})\in\Phi_{NT}
.

It should be noted that constructing a uniformly bounded nuisance estimator is solely for the convenience of subsequent theoretical analysis. In practice, applied researchers do not need to perform this step.

Our construction is based on the following observation:
Since (1)
(\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
is uniformly bounded, (2) the maximum singular value of
G
is uniform bounded, and (3) the minimum singular value of
G
is strictly greater than zero, each entry of
\Lambda_{0}^{G}
and
\Gamma_{0}^{G}
is uniform bound. Thus, we can always find a constant
M>0
that is sufficiently large but independent of
N,T
such that
\|\Lambda_{0}^{G}\|_{\max},\|\Gamma_{0}^{G}\|_{\max}\leq M
wpa1.
We first elaborate on how to obtain the new estimator:

Step 1:

For a large enough constant
M>0
(independent of
N,T
), compute truncated estimators
(\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
, defined as

\displaystyle\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc},ir}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc},ir},&\text{if}\quad|\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc},ir}|\leq M\\
M\mathrm{sign}(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc},ir}),&\text{else }\end{array}\right.

\displaystyle\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc},ir}=\left\{\begin{array}[]{ll}\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc},tr},&\text{if}\quad|\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc},tr}|\leq M\\
M\mathrm{sign}(\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc},tr}),&\text{else }\end{array}\right.

Step 2:

Perform singular value decomposition on
\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\hat{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
, such that
\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}/\sqrt{NT}=\bar{U}\bar{D}\bar{V}
, where
\bar{U}\in\mathbb{R}^{N\times R}
and
\bar{V}\in\mathbb{R}^{T\times R}
are matrices with left and right orthonormal singular vectors of
\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
, and
\bar{D}
is a diagonal matrix with singular values of
\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}/\sqrt{NT}
(arranged in non-increasing order) on its diagonal. We compute
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
as follows:

\displaystyle\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\sqrt{N}\bar{U}\bar{D}^{1/2},\quad\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\sqrt{T}\bar{V}\bar{D}^{1/2}.

(B.8)

By the boundedness of
(\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\Lambda_{0}^{G},\Lambda_{0}^{G})
, we have the following inequalities holds wpa1:

\displaystyle\|\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda_{0}^{G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\|\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda_{0}^{G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{N}B_{1}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right)

(B.9)

\displaystyle\|\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma_{0}^{G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma_{0}^{G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq\sqrt{T}B_{1}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right)

(B.10)

Thus,

\displaystyle\|\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\|\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{\leq M\sqrt{NR}}\|\bar{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma_{0}^{G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}+\underbrace{\|\Gamma_{0}^{G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}}_{\leq M\sqrt{TR}}\|\bar{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda_{0}^{G}\|_{\mathrm{F}}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{2M\sqrt{R}B_{1}}_{B_{2}}\sqrt{NT}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right),\quad\text{wpa1}

Then the difference between the estimation errors of
\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
and
\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
is merely by a constant factor.
Applying the method in Step 3 yields that wpa1

\displaystyle\frac{1}{\sqrt{N}}\|\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Lambda_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}},\frac{1}{\sqrt{T}}\|\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\Gamma_{0}\|_{\mathrm{F}}\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left(\frac{16B_{2}\Omega_{1}^{3/2}}{\Omega_{R}^{2}}+\frac{c_{1}R}{\Omega_{R}^{1/2}}\right)}_{c_{2}}\left(\varphi_{NT}+\frac{\log(NT)}{\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}}\right)

(B.11)

In addition, since
\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
is the product of two rank-
R
uniformly bounded matrices and satisfies
\bar{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}}=\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}}\tilde{\Gamma}^{\prime}_{\mathrm{nuc}}
, each entry in
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
must be uniformly bounded wpa1. When
\rho_{\lambda},\rho_{\gamma}
are sufficiently large (independent of
N,T
), we have
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}})\in\Phi_{NT}
. Therefore, we construct new uniformly bounded nuisance estimators
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
and prove that they achieve the same convergence rate, differing only by a constant.

Since constructing a uniformly bounded nuisance estimator is solely for the convenience of subsequent theoretical analysis, we do not distinguish between
(\hat{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
and
(\tilde{\Lambda}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\tilde{\Gamma}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
in the rest of the paper, with a bit of abuse of notation.

Proof of Lemma
B.2
.

The proof is standard in the literature. Since
(\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
solves the nuclear norm regularized optimization problem (
3.1
), we have

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\beta},\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta})-\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\leq\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}(\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}})

(B.12)

Consider the first-order Taylor expansion of
\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\hat{\beta}_{\mathrm{nuc}},\hat{\Theta}_{\mathrm{nuc}})
. Since
\mathcal{L}_{NT}
is a convex function, we have the following inequality:

\displaystyle\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\beta},\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta})-\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\geq\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}+\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle

(B.13)

Combining (
B.12
) and (
B.13
) yields:

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}(\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}})-\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}-\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle\geq 0

which implies:

\displaystyle\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}(\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}})+|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}|+|\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle|

\displaystyle\geq 0

(B.14)

The term
|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}|
is controlled by:

\displaystyle|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)^{\prime}\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\leq}}\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\varphi_{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|

where inequality (i) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and inequality (ii) holds because of the condition
\varphi_{NT}\geq(1+\alpha)\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|
as stated in Theorem
A.1
. Similarly, we can control
|\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle|
as follows:

\displaystyle|\langle\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right),\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\rangle|\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\leq}}\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|_{\mathrm{op}}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\leq}}\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\frac{\varphi_{NT}}{\sqrt{NT}}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

where inequality (i) follows from the Hölder inequality, as the nuclear norm is the dual norm of the spectral norm, and inequality (ii) holds because of the condition
\varphi_{NT}\geq(1+\alpha)\sqrt{NT}\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}\left(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0}\right)\|_{\mathrm{op}}
as stated in Theorem
A.1
.
Thus, inequality (
B.14
) can be written as:

\displaystyle(\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}})+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\left(\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\right)\geq 0

(B.15)

In addition, we have the following inequalities regarding the nuclear norm:

\displaystyle\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{=}}\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Theta_{0}M_{\Gamma_{0}}+M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\|\Theta_{0}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Theta_{0}M_{\Gamma_{0}}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{=}}\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\|\Theta_{0}+\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(iii)}}}{{\geq}}\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\|\Theta_{0}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

where equality (i) holds due to Lemma
B.1
(i)
, equality (ii) follows from the fact
M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Theta_{0}M_{\Gamma_{0}}=0
, and inequality (iii) is triangular inequality.

Finally, we combine the nuclear norm inequality and (
B.15
) to obtain that:

\displaystyle\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\left(\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|+\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\right)\geq 0

\displaystyle\Rightarrow\frac{2+\alpha}{1+\alpha}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}-\frac{\alpha}{1+\alpha}\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\frac{1}{1+\alpha}\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|\geq 0

\displaystyle\frac{2+\alpha}{\alpha}\left(\|\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}+\sqrt{NT}\|\hat{\Delta}_{\beta}\|\right)\geq\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\hat{\Delta}_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}

This completes the proof.

B.2
Proofs of Corollary
3
and Corollary
A.2

Since Corollary
A.2
extends Corollary
3
to include predetermined covariates, we provide only the proof of Corollary
A.2
, as the proof of the latter can be regarded as a special case.

As stated in Assumption
A.1
(i)
,
\{(Y_{it},W_{it})\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
is
\phi
-mixing with a uniformly exponential decay rate across
i
. However, this assumption is much stronger than required for the proof of Corollary
A.2
. In fact, it can be relaxed to
\{(Y_{it},W_{it})\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
is
\alpha
-mixing with a uniformly sufficiently fast polynomial decay rate across
i
.

Proof of Corollary
A.2
.

It suffices to prove that

\max\{\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\|,\sqrt{NT}\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\|_{\mathrm{op}}\}=o_{p}\left(\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

By Assumption
A.1
(i)
and the first order condition,
\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})
is the sum of weakly dependent bounded random vectors with zero means conditional on
(Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0})
. Consequently, we can apply Theorem 1 in
22
to establish that:

\displaystyle\|\nabla_{\beta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\|<\log(NT)/\sqrt{NT},\quad\text{wpa1}

(B.16)

Note that the
(i,t)
entry of the matrix
\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})
is
\dot{\ell}_{it}(X_{it}^{\prime}\beta_{0}+\theta_{0})
, and it is straightforward to check that (1)
\{\dot{\ell}_{it}(X_{it}^{\prime}\beta_{0}+\theta_{0})\}_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}
is independent across
i
and
\phi
-mixing with uniformly exponential decay rate, (2)
\mathbb{E}_{Z,\Lambda_{0},\Gamma_{0}}(\dot{\ell}_{it}(X_{it}^{\prime}\beta_{0}+\theta_{0}))=0
by first-order condition, and (3)
\dot{\ell}_{it}(X_{it}^{\prime}\beta_{0}+\theta_{0})
is uniformly bounded across
i,t,N,T
by Assumption
A.1
(ii)
and Assumption
A.1
(iii)
. Therefore, we can employ Lemma
E.2
to obtain

\displaystyle NT\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\|_{\mathrm{op}}=O_{p}\left(\log(N+T)\sqrt{\max\{N,T\}}/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

and thus,

\displaystyle\sqrt{NT}\|\nabla_{\Theta}\mathcal{L}_{NT}(\beta_{0},\Theta_{0})\|=o_{p}\left(\log(NT)/\sqrt{\min\{N,T\}}\right)

(B.17)

We then combine (
B.16
) and (
B.17
) to complete proof.

B.3
Proof of Lemma
A.4

Our proof builds on
13
(see Lemma D.3 and Lemma D.4 in its Appendix) and extends it to accommodate serial correlation. The extension introduces additional technical complexity, particularly in deriving the high-probability upper bound for the empirical process under weak dependence. To address this problem, we apply the concentration inequality in
34
to establish the concentration bound around the expectation of the empirical process. Furthermore, we use the block method introduced by
41
, constructing a new sequence with independent blocks to approximate the original sequence. When the block size is sufficiently large, the correlation between separated blocks becomes negligible, thus facilitating the theoretical analysis.

It is worth noting that our proof strategy is not only applicable to models with homogeneous slopes, but with minor modifications, it can also be extended to accommodate heterogeneous slopes (e.g.,
13
,
24
). We believe the flexibility of our strategy enhances the applicability of our approach to a broader class of models.

Proof of Lemma
A.4
.
Recall the definition of the constraints space:

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{1}=\left\{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times T})\mid\|M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Delta_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\leq c_{0}\left(\sqrt{NT}\|\Delta_{\beta}\|+\|\Delta_{\Theta}-M_{\Lambda_{0}}\Delta_{\Theta}M_{\Gamma_{0}}\|_{\mathrm{nuc}}\right)\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{C}_{2}=\left\{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in(\mathbb{R}^{d_{X}}\times\mathbb{R}^{N\times T})\mid\|\Delta_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}\|\Delta_{\Theta}\|_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\geq\sqrt{\frac{\log(NT)}{NT}}\right\}

For notational simplicity, let
\mathcal{C}=\mathcal{C}_{1}\cap\mathcal{C}_{2}
. Also, use
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathbb{E}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{V})
to denote the conditional expectation, and
\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{V}}=\mathbb{P}(\cdot\mid\mathcal{V})
to denote the conditional probability.

Step 1

In this step, we aim to establish a lower bound for
\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}
. By Assumption
A.3
(ii)
, there exist a constant
\kappa_{0}>0
such that:

\displaystyle\inf_{1\leq i\leq N,1\leq t\leq T}\sigma_{\min}\left(\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}X_{it}^{\prime})&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it})\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}^{\prime})&1\end{pmatrix}\right)\geq\kappa_{0}>0

It follows that:

\displaystyle\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}

\displaystyle\geq\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta^{\prime}_{\beta}&\Delta_{\theta_{it}}\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}X_{it}^{\prime})&\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it})\\
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}^{\prime})&1\end{pmatrix}\begin{pmatrix}\Delta_{\beta}\\
\Delta_{\theta_{it}}\end{pmatrix}

(B.18)

\displaystyle\geq\kappa_{0}\left(NT\|\Delta_{\beta}\|^{2}+{|\kern-1.07639pt|\Delta_{\Theta}|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\right)

Step 2 (Concentration around expectation)

For any
\omega>0
, define the constraint set
\mathcal{N}(\omega)
:

\displaystyle\mathcal{N}(\omega)=\left\{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{C}\mid\|\Delta_{\beta}\|^{2}+\frac{1}{NT}{|\kern-1.07639pt|\Delta_{\Theta}|\kern-1.07639pt|}_{\mathrm{F}}^{2}\leq\omega,\text{ }\|\Delta_{\beta}\|_{\max}\leq 2\rho_{\beta},\text{ }\|\Delta_{\Theta}\|_{\max}\leq 2\rho_{\theta}\right\}

and define the empirical process:

\displaystyle Z(\omega)=\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right|

It is straightforward to check that:

\displaystyle\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}|(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}|

\displaystyle\leq 2\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{B}(\omega)}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}

\displaystyle\leq 4\sup_{\|\Delta_{\beta}\|_{\max}\leq 2\rho_{\beta},{|\kern-0.75346pt|\Delta_{\theta}|\kern-0.75346pt|}_{\max}\leq 2\rho_{\theta}}\left\{(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta})^{2}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}}^{2}\right\}

\displaystyle\leq\underbrace{16(d_{X}\rho_{X}^{2}\rho_{\beta}^{2}+\rho_{\theta}^{2})}_{\sigma}

almost surely. Additionally, by the Efron-Stein inequality, we have:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\ \sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t=1}^{T}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left((X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right)^{2}\leq\frac{NT\sigma^{2}}{4}

By Assumption
A.3
(i)
, conditional on
\mathcal{V}
, the sequence
\{X_{it}\}_{1\leq t\leq T}
is
\phi
-mixing with an exponential decay rate uniformly across
i
, This allows us to directly apply Theorem 3 from
34
to obtain the following concentration inequality of
Z(\omega)
around its expectation:

\displaystyle\mathbb{P}_{\mathcal{V}}\left(Z(\omega)\geq\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z(\omega)+\delta\right)\leq\exp\left(L^{-1}\min\left\{\frac{\delta}{\sigma},\frac{\delta^{2}}{NT\sigma^{2}}\right\}\right),\quad\forall\delta>0

where
1\leq L<\infty
that does not depend on
N,T
, and is only determined by the mixing property of
X_{it}
conditional on
\mathcal{V}
.

Step 3 (Upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z(\omega)
)

We follow
41
’s block method to provide an upper bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z(\omega)
. Define
X_{i}=\left(X_{i1},X_{i2},\ldots,X_{iT}\right)
,
where
\{X_{i}\}
is independent across
i
, and for each
i
,
X_{i}
is
\phi
-mixing with mixing coefficients
\phi(\tau)
. Let
\tau_{NT}\leq T
be a positive integer, and
\mu_{NT}=\lfloor\frac{T}{2\tau_{NT}}\rfloor
be the largest integer less than or equal to
\frac{T}{2\tau_{NT}}
. For each
X_{i}
, we divide the sequence into
2\mu_{NT}
blocks, each of length
\tau_{NT}
, with the remaining term part having a length of at most
2\tau_{NT}
. The subscript notation indicates that the values of
\tau_{NT}
and
\mu_{NT}
may depend on
N
and
T
.

We further partition the blocks into two groups: odd-numbered blocks and even-numbered blocks. For notational simplicity, let
\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{k}
denote the indices of elements in the
k
-th odd block, and
\mathcal{T}^{(0)}
be the set of indices of all elements contained in odd-numbered blocks. Similarly, let
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}_{k}
be indices of elements in the
k
-th even block, and
\mathcal{T}^{(1)}
be set of the indices of all elements in even-numbered blocks. Specifically:

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(0)}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{k},\quad\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{k}=\left\{t\mid 2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+1\leq t\leq 2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau_{NT}\right\}

\displaystyle\mathcal{T}^{(1)}=\bigcup_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\mathcal{T}^{(1)}_{k},\quad\mathcal{T}^{(1)}_{k}=\left\{t\mid(2k-1)\tau_{NT}+1\leq t\leq(2k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau_{NT}\right\}

The corresponding partition of
X_{i}
can be written as:

\displaystyle X_{i}^{(0)}=\left(X_{i}^{(0,1)},X_{i}^{(0,2)},\ldots,X_{i}^{(0,\mu_{NT})}\right)

\displaystyle X_{i}^{(1)}=\left(X_{i}^{(1,1)},X_{i}^{(1,2)},\ldots,X_{i}^{(1,\mu_{NT})}\right)

where for each
1\leq k\leq\mu_{NT}
,

\displaystyle X_{i}^{(0,k)}=\left(X_{it}\mid t\in\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{k}\right),\quad X_{i}^{(1,k)}=\left(X_{it}\mid t\in\mathcal{T}^{(1)}_{k}\right)

and the remaining term can be collected into
R_{i}
:

\displaystyle R_{i}=\left(X_{it}\mid t\in\mathcal{R}\right),\quad\mathcal{R}=\left\{t\mid 2\tau_{NT}\mu_{NT}+1\leq t\leq T\right\}

In the next step, for each
i
, we construct a new sequence with an independent block structure conditional on
\mathcal{V}
:

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(0)}_{i}=\left(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(0,1)},\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(0,2)},\ldots,\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(0,\mu_{NT})}\right)

such that each block
\widetilde{X}^{(0,k)}_{i}
(of size
\tau_{NT}
) is independent with each other conditional on
\mathcal{V}
, but within each block,
\widetilde{X}^{(0,k)}_{i}
conforms the same conditional distribution as in
X^{(0,k)}_{i}
.
We then construct
\{\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{i}\}
in the similar way:

\displaystyle\widetilde{X}^{(1)}_{i}=\left(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(1,1)},\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(1,2)},\ldots,\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(1,\mu_{NT})}\right)

where each block is independent with each other conditional on
\mathcal{V}
, but within each block,
\widetilde{X}^{(1,k)}_{i}
follows the same conditional distribution as in
X^{(1,k)}_{i}
.

Denote
\widetilde{X}=(\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(0,1)},\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(1,1)},\ldots,\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(0,\mu_{NT})},\widetilde{X}_{i}^{(1,\mu_{NT})})
, and for
s\in\{0,1\}
, define the empirical process as:

\displaystyle Z^{(s)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\widetilde{Z}^{(s)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(\widetilde{X}_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(\widetilde{X}_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right|

It is straightforward to see that the expectation of supreme
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z(\omega)
can be bounded as follows:

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z(\omega)\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(0)}(\omega)+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(1)}(\omega)

(B.19)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(0)}(\omega)+\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}(\omega)+|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(0)}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(0)}(\omega)|+|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(1)}(\omega)|

\displaystyle+2\sigma N\tau_{NT}

where the last term on the right-hand side of the inequality,
2\sigma N\tau_{NT}
arises due to the remaining terms when T cannot be exactly divided by
\tau_{NT}
. The following lemma is crucial in bounding
|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(0)}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(0)}(\omega)|
and
|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(1)}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(1)}(\omega)|
:

Lemma B.3
(Lemma 4.1 in
41
)

For any measurable function
h
on
\mathbb{R}^{N\times\tau_{NT}\mu_{NT}}
with bound
M>0
, we have

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}h(X^{(s)}_{1},X^{(s)}_{2},\ldots,X^{(s)}_{N})-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}h(\widetilde{X}^{(s)}_{1},\widetilde{X}^{(s)}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{X}^{(s)}_{N})|\leq M\left(N\mu_{NT}-1\right)\phi(\tau_{NT}),\quad s=0,1

To apply Lemma
B.3
, let

\displaystyle h^{(s)}(X^{(s)}_{1},X^{(s)}_{2},\ldots,X^{(s)}_{N})=\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right|

and it is easy to show that the following inequality holds almost surely for
s=0,1
:

\displaystyle|h^{(s)}(X^{(s)}_{1},X^{(s)}_{2},\ldots,X^{(s)}_{N})|\leq

\displaystyle 2\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta})^{2}+\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}\Delta_{\theta_{it}}^{2}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(X_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta})^{2}+4\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}\Delta_{\theta_{it}}^{2}

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 4N\mu_{NT}\tau_{NT}d_{X}\rho_{X}^{2}\omega+4NT\omega

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2NT(d_{X}\rho_{X}^{2}+2)\omega

Thus, applying Lemma
B.3
on
|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(s)}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(s)}(\omega)|
(with
M\leq 2NT(d_{X}\rho_{X}^{2}+2)\omega
) yields

\displaystyle|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(s)}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(s)}(\omega)|\leq

\displaystyle 2NT\left(d_{X}\rho_{X}^{2}+2\right)\omega\left(N\mu_{NT}-1\right)\phi(\tau_{NT})

(B.20)

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\underbrace{\left(d_{X}\rho_{X}^{2}+2\right)}_{\frac{C_{0}}{2}}(NT)^{2}\frac{\phi(\tau_{NT})}{\tau_{NT}}\omega

Intuitively, when
\phi(\tau_{NT})
decays fast enough as
\tau_{NT}\rightarrow\infty
, we can expect that
\phi(\tau_{NT})/\tau_{NT}\rightarrow 0
fast enough so that the
|\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(s)}(\omega)-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}Z^{(s)}(\omega)|
can be well bounded.

We now turn to establish a bound for
\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(s)}(\omega)
. Since each block in
\widetilde{X}_{i}
is independent with each other conditional on
\mathcal{V}
, we can study the Rademacher process corresponding to each block. More specifically, for each
s
, we can construct a set of i.i.d. Rademacher random variables
\{\epsilon_{ik}^{(s)}\mid i=1,2,\ldots,N,k=1,2,\ldots,\mu_{NT}\}
, which is independent of
(\widetilde{X}^{(s)}_{1},\widetilde{X}^{(s)}_{2},\ldots,\widetilde{X}^{(s)}_{N})
conditional on
\mathcal{V}
. Using symmetrization method, we have

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\widetilde{Z}^{(s)}(\omega)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(\widetilde{X}_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}-\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}}(\widetilde{X}_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right|

(B.21)

\displaystyle\leq 2\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\left(\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(s)}_{k}}(\widetilde{X}_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right)\epsilon_{ik}^{(s)}\right|

For
s=0
, we have

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\left(\sum_{t\in\mathcal{T}^{(0)}_{k}}(\widetilde{X}_{it}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{it}})^{2}\right)\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right|

(B.22)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle 2\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{NT}}\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}(\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}})^{2}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle 2\sum_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{NT}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}(\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}})^{2}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\leq}}

\displaystyle\underbrace{16(d_{X}\rho_{X}\rho_{\beta}+\rho_{\theta})}_{\frac{C_{1}}{2}}\sum_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{NT}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}(\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}+\Delta_{\theta_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}})\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right|

\displaystyle\leq

\displaystyle\frac{C_{1}}{2}\sum_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{NT}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right|}_{S_{1,\tau}}

\displaystyle+\frac{C_{1}}{2}\sum_{\tau=1}^{\tau_{NT}}\underbrace{\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\Delta_{\theta_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right|}_{S_{2,\tau}}

where we change the order of summation to obtain inequality (i). Inequality (ii) follows from the contraction property of the Rademacher process (see Section 4.2 in
23
). To give an upper bound of
S_{2,\tau}
, for each
\tau
,

\displaystyle S_{1,\tau}

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\Delta_{\beta}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right|

(B.23)

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(i)}}}{{\leq}}\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left(\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right\|\|\Delta_{\beta}\|\right)

\displaystyle=\mathbb{E}_{\mathcal{V},\epsilon}\left\|\sum_{i=1}^{N}\sum_{k=1}^{\mu_{NT}}\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}\right\|\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\|\Delta_{\beta}\|

\displaystyle\stackrel{{\scriptstyle\text{(ii)}}}{{\leq}}\underbrace{\sqrt{8\pi d_{X}^{3}\rho_{X}^{2}}}_{\sqrt{2}C_{2}}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\|\Delta_{\beta}\|

\displaystyle\leq\sqrt{2}C_{2}\sqrt{N\mu_{NT}}\sqrt{\omega}

\displaystyle\leq C_{2}\sqrt{\frac{NT\omega}{\tau_{NT}}}

where inequality (i) follows from the Cauchy-Schwarz inequality, and inequality (ii) is from Lemma
E.1
using the fact that each element in
\widetilde{X}_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}^{\prime}\epsilon_{ik}^{(0)}
is bounded by
\rho_{X}
.

Let
\Delta^{(0,\tau)}_{\Theta}
be an
N\times\mu_{NT}
matrix such that
[\Delta^{(0,\tau)}_{\Theta}]_{ik}=\theta_{i,2(k-1)\tau_{NT}+\tau}
for each
\tau=1,2,\ldots,\tau_{NT}
. Let
E^{(0)}
denote the
N\times\mu_{NT}
matrix collecting
\epsilon^{(s)}_{ik}
, we can show that

\displaystyle S_{2,\tau}=

\displaystyle\mathbb{E}_{\epsilon}\sup_{(\Delta_{\beta},\Delta_{\Theta})\in\mathcal{N}(\omega)}\left|\langle\Delta_{\Theta}^{(0,\tau)},E^{(0)}\rangle\right|

(B.24)

\displaystyle\leq
